# A cofibrant model of Waldhausen K-theory

**arXiv ID**: 2607.10886v1
**Authors**: Kate Ponto, Inna Zakharevich
**Published**: 2026-07-12
**Categories**: math.KT, math.AT, math.CT
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.10886v1

## Abstract

This paper gives some technical results about cofibrant symmetric spectra of simplicial sets, and uses them to produce a model of the $K$-theory of a Waldhausen category that lands in the subcategory of (projective) cofibrant spectra.

## A cofibrant model of WaldhausenKK-theoryKate PontoandInna Zakharevich

## Abstract.

This paper gives some technical results about cofibrant symmetric spectra of
simplicial sets, and uses them to produce a model of theKK-theory of a
Waldhausen category that lands in the subcategory of (projective) cofibrant
spectra.

## Introduction

When working with algebraicKK-theory there is often a tension of approaches.
On one hand, working with∞\infty-categories and abstract approaches produces
satisfying and beautiful results, such as Blumberg–Gepner–Tabuada’s result
about the universal property ofKK-theory[BGT13]. In
this approach it is not important to construct models, and this often
strengthens and streamlines results. Unfortunately, sometimes (such as in cases
where there is not a stable∞\infty-categorical structure, such as
combinatorialKK-theory[Zak17,BGM+24]) it is necessary to work
directly with explicit constructions ofKK-theory, such as Waldhausen’sS∙S_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}-construction. Unfortunately, with such direct constructions we often
run into problems: we must work with model structures, and thus we often need to
know that the spectra that we are working with are cofibrant.

Waldhausen’sS∙S_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}-construction naturally produces a symmetric spectrum of
simplicial sets. Symmetric spectra of simplicial sets have three main model
structures on them: the level model structure, the flat model structure, and the
projective model structure. These have decreasing collections of cofibrations,
with the projective model structure being the one most useful for many
applications.
It is thus desirable to know when theS∙S_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}-construction
produces projectively cofibrant spectra.

## Theorem A(Proposition2.4, Corollary2.5).

For a Waldhausen category𝒞\mathcal{C}, theS∙S_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}-construction produces a
symmetric spectrum of simplicial setsK​(𝒞)K(\mathcal{C})which is flat but not projective
cofibrant.

This is unfortunate, but it turns out that it is possible to fix it.

Moreover, both of these functors behave well on “injective” functors:

faithful and injective on objects. ThenK​(F)K(F)is a flat cofibration andK′​(F)K^{\prime}(F)is a projective cofibration.

We prove these results by developing an explicit description of flat cofibrant
spectra (Proposition4.9).
With this explicit description we strengthen the cofibrancy results to simplicial spectra, as
well. This is useful in particular when taking realizations of symmetric
spectra: since a realization is a homotopy colimit, knowing that the spectrum is
Reedy projective cofibrant implies that the realization is projective cofibrant.

## Theorem D(Proposition3.8).

For a simplicial Waldhausen category𝒞∙\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}, the simplicial spectrumK​(𝒞∙)K(\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}})is Reedy cofibrant in the flat model structure, andK′​(𝒞∙)K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}})is Reedy cofibrant in the projective model structure.
Consequently, their geometric realizations are cofibrant in the flat and
projective model structures, respectively.

## Organization

Section1introduces the concept oflatchingandincreasing spectra, which are the foundational concepts for proving the
main theorems. It also states the main technical results about increasing
spectra that are needed to prove the main theorems, including the foundational
claim that increasing spectra are exactly flat spectra.
Section2uses the results of
Section1to prove the first three main theorems aboutKK-theory of Waldhausen categories; Section3discusses the application to simplicial Waldhausen categories.
Section4proves that a spectrum is increasing if and only if
it is flat, modulo some
technical combinatorial results that are proved in
Section5.

## Notation and conventions

In this paper we work exclusively with symmetric spectra of simplicial sets; we do not specify “of
simplicial sets” anywhere beyond this section.

𝕊\mathbb{S}denotes the sphere spectrumS0,S1,S1∧S1,S1∧S1∧S1,…S^{0},S^{1},S^{1}\wedge S^{1},S^{1}\wedge S^{1}\wedge S^{1},\ldots, with identity morphisms as the structure maps.

The symmetric group is denotedΣn\Sigma_{n}.

## 1.Foundational technical results

In this section we state the essential technical results of the paper. As they
are easy to state and apply, but laborious to prove, we start with the
application and postpone the proofs to Sections4and5.

The spectrum𝕊¯\overline{\mathbb{S}}, thetruncated
sphere spectrum, is defined to be the spectrum which has a single point in
degree0and thenSqS^{q}in degreeqqforq>0q>0.

## Remark 1.4.

For the purpose of this paper, it is sufficient to know that cofibrations ofΣn\Sigma_{n}-simplicial sets are exactly the monomorphisms (this is stated in[Sch, Below Definition III.4.1]), and that the freeΣn\Sigma_{n}-cofibrations are those cofibrations where theΣn\Sigma_{n}-action is
free on the complement of the image (see[GJ99, Proposition V.2.4, Corollary
V.2.10], applied to the case whereG∙G_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}is constant).

Before beginning to analyze the structure of spectra, we will need several
facts about shuffles.

## Definition 1.5.

A permutationτ∈Σn\tau\in\Sigma_{n}is an(n−k,k)(n-k,k)-shuffleifτ​(1)<⋯<τ​(n−k)\tau(1)<\cdots<\tau(n-k)andτ​(n−k+1)<⋯<τ​(n)\tau(n-k+1)<\cdots<\tau(n).

The following are the basic facts about shuffles that we will be using:

## Lemma 1.6.
- (1)

The(n−k,k)(n-k,k)-shuffles represent the cosets ofΣn−k×Σk\Sigma_{n-k}\times\Sigma_{k}inΣn\Sigma_{n}. In other words, for anyγ∈Σn\gamma\in\Sigma_{n}there exists a unique factorizationγ=γ′​(τ,ρ)\gamma=\gamma^{\prime}(\tau,\rho), whereγ′\gamma^{\prime}is an(n−k,k)(n-k,k)-shuffle and(τ,ρ)∈Σn−k×Σk(\tau,\rho)\in\Sigma_{n-k}\times\Sigma_{k}.
- (2)

For a(k,n−k)(k,n-k)-shuffleγ\gammaand an(ℓ,k−ℓ)(\ell,k-\ell)-shuffleτ\tau,
there is a unique(ℓ,n−ℓ)(\ell,n-\ell)-shuffleγ′\gamma^{\prime}and a uniqueρ∈Σn−ℓ\rho\in\Sigma_{n-\ell}such thatγ​(τ,1)=γ′​(1,ρ)\gamma(\tau,1)=\gamma^{\prime}(1,\rho).

We are now ready to definelatched simplices.

Whenτ\tauandδ\deltaare clear from context (or unimportant) we simply say thatxxis latched toyy.
Further, we say a simplexxxislatchedif there is a tuple(k,y,δ,τ)(k,y,\delta,\tau)so thaty∈Xky\in X_{k}is(δ,τ)(\delta,\tau)-latched toxx, otherwise we say thatxxisunlatched.

LetΛn​X\Lambda_{n}Xbe the full simplicial subset of latched simplices.

## Notation 1.8.

We will writeσ\sigmainstead ofσk\sigma_{k}for structure
maps whenkkis clear from context. Thus for a simplexδ∈Sk\delta\in S^{k}, we
writeσ​(x,δ)\sigma(x,\delta)to meanσk​(x,δ)\sigma_{k}(x,\delta).

## Lemma 1.9.

Λn​X\Lambda_{n}Xis a well-definedΣn\Sigma_{n}-simplicial subset ofXnX_{n}.

## Proof.

By direct computationΛn​X\Lambda_{n}Xis a well-defined simplicial subset, because
ifxxis(δ,τ)(\delta,\tau)-latched toy∈Xk−1y\in X_{k-1}thendi​xd_{i}xis(di​δ,τ)(d_{i}\delta,\tau)-latched todi​yd_{i}yandsi​xs_{i}xis(si​δ,τ)(s_{i}\delta,\tau)-latched tosi​ys_{i}y.

Now we show that theΣn\Sigma_{n}-action is well-defined.
We must show that ifx∈Λn​Xx\in\Lambda_{n}Xandρ∈Σn\rho\in\Sigma_{n}thenρ⋅x∈Λn​X\rho\cdot x\in\Lambda_{n}X. Suppose thatxxis(τ,δ)(\tau,\delta)-latched toyy, so thatτ⋅σk​(y,δ)=x\tau\cdot\sigma_{k}(y,\delta)=x. Then(ρ​τ)⋅σk​(y,δ)=ρ⋅x(\rho\tau)\cdot\sigma_{k}(y,\delta)=\rho\cdot x. Since the shuffles
represent the left cosets ofΣn−k×Σk\Sigma_{n-k}\times\Sigma_{k}inΣn\Sigma_{n},
there exists a unique shuffleτ′\tau^{\prime}and(ρn−k,ρk)∈Σn−k×Σk(\rho_{n-k},\rho_{k})\in\Sigma_{n-k}\times\Sigma_{k}such thatρ​τ=τ′​(ρn−k,ρk)\rho\tau=\tau^{\prime}(\rho_{n-k},\rho_{k}). Thenρ⋅x=τ′​σk​(ρn−k​y,ρk​δ).\rho\cdot x=\tau^{\prime}\sigma_{k}(\rho_{n-k}y,\rho_{k}\delta).

Thusρ​x\rho xis(τ′,ρk​δ)(\tau^{\prime},\rho_{k}\delta)-latched toρn−k​y\rho_{n-k}y, and soρ​x∈Λn​X\rho x\in\Lambda_{n}X, as desired.
∎

The point ofΛn\Lambda_{n}is that it is a more hands-on definition of the image
of the traditional latching object.

## Lemma 1.10.

For any spectrumXX,νT​(LnT​X)=Λn​X\nu^{T}(L^{T}_{n}X)=\Lambda_{n}X.

## Proof.

Suppose thatx∈Λn​Xx\in\Lambda_{n}X. Then there exists ann−1n-1-simplexyysuch
thatxxis(δ,τ)(\delta,\tau)-latched toyy. But thenxxis in the image of(τ,y,δ)∈LnT​X(\tau,y,\delta)\in L^{T}_{n}X, and thus is in the image ofLnT​XL^{T}_{n}X; thusΛn​X⊆νT​(LnT​X)\Lambda_{n}X\subseteq\nu^{T}(L^{T}_{n}X). On the other
hand, consider a simplex represented by(τ,y,δ)∈LnT​X(\tau,y,\delta)\in L^{T}_{n}X. ThenνT​(τ,y,δ)=τ⋅σ​(y,δ)\nu^{T}(\tau,y,\delta)=\tau\cdot\sigma(y,\delta), and thus its image inXnX_{n}is inΛn​X\Lambda_{n}X. ThusνT​(LnT​X)⊆Λn​X\nu^{T}(L^{T}_{n}X)\subseteq\Lambda_{n}X, and
the two are equal, as desired.
∎

In many cases of interest (in particular, theKK-theoretic examples we are
working with), the structure maps inXXare all monic, with symmetric actions
being generated somewhat “freely,” by which we mean that structure maps are
monic (and thus do not lose information) and the symmetric group action does not
“repeat information”. We encode this in the definition of anincreasing
spectrum.

## Theorem 1.13.

A spectrum is flat if and only if it is increasing.

## 2.CofibrantKK-theory

The goal of this section is to give a construction ofK​(𝒞)K(\mathcal{C})for a Waldhausen
category𝒞\mathcal{C}which will give a symmetric spectrum which is cofibrant in the
projective stable model structure on symmetric spectra.

In this section,𝒞\mathcal{C}will always be a Waldhausen category. We will refer to
the “all-at-once”KK-theory construction of[BM11, Construction
2.2]as theusualS∙S_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}-construction. This
construction produces a symmetric spectrum. We use the notation of[BM11, Construction 2.2], except that we simplify their
subscriptsn1,…,nqn_{1},\ldots,n_{q}asn→\vec{n}. In order for this construction to be
well-defined as a symmetric spectrum it is necessary to have a distinguished
zero object, and thus many people assume that there is a unique zero object in a
Waldhausen category. We instead make the assumption that𝒞\mathcal{C}ispointed: that there is achosenzero object that is distinguished,
but that other zero objects may exist.

## Example 2.2.

In the context of Waldhausen’sS∙S_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}-construction,Λn​K​(𝒞)\Lambda_{n}K(\mathcal{C})can be

## Lemma 2.3.

For alln≥0n\geq 0,Λn​K​(𝒞)⊆K​(𝒞)n\Lambda_{n}K(\mathcal{C})\subseteq K(\mathcal{C})_{n}is the subspace of
those diagramsAAfor which there exists some1≤i≤n1\leq i\leq nso thatAAisii-trivial.

## Proof.

In the usual construction forK​(𝒞)K(\mathcal{C}), the structure mapσ\sigmatakes a
diagram of dimensionn−1n-1and creates a diagram of dimensionnnwhich isnn-trivial. (See, for example,[Zak18, Section 6]for a discussion
of this construction in detail.) TheΣn\Sigma_{n}-action on diagramsAr⁡[m→]\operatorname{Ar}[\vec{m}]permutes the coordinates ofAr⁡[m→]\operatorname{Ar}[\vec{m}]; so if a
diagramAAisii-trivial for alli∈Ii\in I(for someI⊆{1,…,q}I\subseteq\{1,\ldots,q\}) then the diagramτ⋅A\tau\cdot Awill beτ​(i)\tau(i)-trivial for alli∈Ii\in I. In particular, all latched simplices must
beii-trivial for someii.
∎

Using this characterization we can show thatK​(𝒞)K(\mathcal{C})is flat.

## Proposition 2.4.

For the usualS∙S_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}-construction,K​(𝒞)K(\mathcal{C})is increasing, and therefore
flat.

## Proof.

Since all of the data of a diagram is preserved byσ1\sigma_{1},σ1\sigma_{1}is
monic and thus all structure maps ofK​(𝒞)K(\mathcal{C})are monic. Moreover, ifρ\rhohasρ​(n)≠n\rho(n)\neq nandσ​(x,δ)=ρ⋅σ​(x′,δ′)\sigma(x,\delta)=\rho\cdot\sigma(x^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})then this implies that the diagram represented byxxwasnn-trivial andρ​(n)\rho(n)-trivial. In particular, if we take the subdiagram in which thenn-th andρ​(n)\rho(n)-th coordinates are both set to the maximum value, we get
ann−2n-2-simplex that bothxxandx′x^{\prime}are latched to.
ThusK​(𝒞)K(\mathcal{C})is increasing, and flat by
Theorem1.13.
∎

For our construction of cofibrantKK-theory we require that each object in the category has
infinitely many objects that are isomorphic. If necessary we replace the category with an equivalent one as in the following definition.

In order to produce a model ofKK-theory where fixed points such as the one in
Example2.2do not occur, we wish to restrict attention to those
diagrams with all entries distinct. The point of considering𝒞ℤ~\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}instead of𝒞\mathcal{C}is to ensure, in a functorial manner, that there exist enough copies of
each object to make this possible, and to make sure that even when a functor is
not injective on objects, it will take diagrams with nonrepeating entries to
diagrams with nonrepeating entries.

LetS~n→(q)​𝒞ℤ~\widetilde{S}^{(q)}_{\vec{n}}\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}}be the full Waldhausen subcategory ofSn→(q)​(𝒞ℤ~)S^{(q)}_{\vec{n}}(\mathcal{C}_{\widetilde{\mathbb{Z}}})containing only essentially nonrepeating
diagrams.

Since repetitions in an essentially nonrepeating diagram can be obtained only
through the application of degeneracies, it can be shown by induction that the
only way for an object to repeat in an essentially nonrepeating diagram is in a
contiguous rectangle.

## Corollary 2.13.

The mapι\iotais a weak equivalence.

Proposition2.10implies thatι\iotais a level equivalence,

As a first step to showing thatK′​(𝒞)K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})is projective we will show that it is
flat. The following is proved identically to Proposition2.4.

## Lemma 2.14.

For the cofibrant construction ofKK-theory,K′​(𝒞)K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})is
increasing and therefore flat.

## Remark 2.15.

Exactly as observed in Lemma2.3, fork≥1k\geq 1, the
spaceΛk​K′​(𝒞)\Lambda_{k}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})is the subspace ofK′​(𝒞)kK^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})_{k}which contains those
diagrams which areii-trivial for some1≤i≤k1\leq i\leq k.

We turn our attention to proving thatK′​(𝒞)K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})is projective. To prove this we
need a preliminary result showing that the simplices which makeK​(𝒞)K(\mathcal{C})fail to
be projective are not present insideK′​(𝒞)K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}).

objects. ThenK′​(F)K^{\prime}(F)is a projective cofibration.

## Proof.

By Corollary2.17K′​(𝒟)K^{\prime}(\mathcal{D})is projective; by definitionK​(F)K(F)is
a level cofibration. We now show thatK′​(F)K^{\prime}(F)is flat.
Following the same lines as the proof of Proposition2.6, we see
that it suffices to show thatK′​(F)n​(K′​(𝒞)n)∩Λn​K′​(𝒟)⊆K′​(F)n​(Λn​K′​(𝒞)).K^{\prime}(F)_{n}(K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})_{n})\cap\Lambda_{n}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{D})\subseteq K^{\prime}(F)_{n}(\Lambda_{n}K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C})).

By the same logic as in that proof, this is true. ThusK′​(F)K^{\prime}(F)is a flat
cofibration; by Corollary1.15,
it is also a projective cofibration.
∎

## 3.TheKK-theory of simplicial Waldhausen categories

The goal of this section is to show thatK′K^{\prime}behaves well with respect
to the geometric realization of simplicial spectra. In particular, our goal is
to prove the following theorem:

## Theorem 3.1.

Let𝒞∙\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}be a simplicial object in
the category of Waldhausen categories. Then, in the projective (resp. flat)
model structure on symmetric spectra,|K′​(𝒞∙)||K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}})|(resp.|K​(𝒞∙)||K(\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}})|)
is cofibrant.

We begin with a review of Reedy cofibrancy of simplicial objects in a model
category.

## Example 3.3.

When𝒞=𝐒𝐞𝐭\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Set}andX∙X_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}is a simplicial set,Lnℛ​X∙L_{n}^{\mathcal{R}}X_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}is the
subset ofXnX_{n}given by the degenerate simplices. If𝒞=s​𝐒𝐞𝐭\mathcal{C}=s\mathbf{Set}andX∙X_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}is a bisimplicial set, thenLnℛ​X∙L_{n}^{\mathcal{R}}X_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}is the subspace ofXnX_{n}given by the union of the images ofsi​Xn−1s_{i}X_{n-1}for0≤i≤n−10\leq i\leq n-1.

The reason that we can just take the union of the images is that all of the
mapssis_{i}have left inverses, and intersect in predictable ways. In
particular, suppose thatsi​y=sj​zs_{i}y=s_{j}zfor some simplicesyyandzz. Then
ifi=ji=jwe must havey=zy=z, by applyingdid_{i}on the left. Ifi<ji<jthen
we must havey=sj−1​di​zy=s_{j-1}d_{i}zandz=si​di​zz=s_{i}d_{i}z. Thus in the colimit
definingLnℛ​X∙L_{n}^{\mathcal{R}}X_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}, simplices whose image are equal inXnX_{n}are
already equal.

## Example 3.4.

Let𝒞=𝐒𝐩\mathcal{C}=\mathbf{Sp}. ThenLnℛ​X∙L_{n}^{\mathcal{R}}X_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}is the subspectrum ofXnX_{n}which, at
levelkkis the union of the images of the spacessi​(Xn−1)ks_{i}(X_{n-1})_{k}. (This
uses Example3.3and the fact that colimits in𝐒𝐩\mathbf{Sp}are
levelwise.)

## Remark 3.5.

The standard term for the Reedy latching object is simply “latching object”,
and it is denoted simplyLnL_{n}; as this would lead to ambiguity in our
notation, we have renamed it.

## Example 3.7.

By the calculation in Example3.3, all bisimplicial sets are
Reedy cofibrant, and more generally all pointwise monomorphisms of
bisimplicial sets are Reedy cofibrations. (See[GJ99, Theorem
IV.3.9]for an in-depth discussion of this example.)

The main goal of this section is to analyze the Reedy latching objects of theKK-theory of simplicial Waldhausen categories and thereby prove that theKK-theory of their homotopy colimits are cofibrant. We begin with the
following characterization of the Reedy latching object.

The main technical result needed is the following:

## Proposition 3.8.

Let𝒞∙\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}be a simplicial
object in the category of Waldhausen categories. ThenK​(𝒞∙)K(\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}})is flat
Reedy cofibrant andK′​(𝒞∙)K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}})is projective Reedy cofibrant.

## Proof.

We present the argument for the projective case, as the proof for the flat
case is a minor simplification of the projective case (after replacing each
instance ofK′K^{\prime}withKK).

∎

Given this result, we are ready to prove the theorem.

## Proof of Theorem3.1.

By Proposition3.8,K′​(𝒞∙)K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}})(resp.K​(𝒞∙)K(\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}})) is
Reedy cofibrant for the projective (resp. flat) model structure on symmetric
spectra.

By[Sch, Theorem III.4.11], the category of symmetric spectra of
simplicial sets, with either the flat or the projective stable model
structure, are simplicial. By[GJ99, Proposition VII.3.6], the
geometric realization of simplicial objects in a simplicial model category
preserves cofibrations between Reedy cofibrant objects. Thus|K′​(𝒞∙)||K^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}})|(resp.|K​(𝒞∙)||K(\mathcal{C}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}})|) is cofibrant, as desired.
∎

Lastly, we want to mimic the results of Proposition2.6and
Corollary2.19, for the simplicial Waldhausen category case.

based on the flat (resp. projective) stable model structure on symmetric
spectra. Consequently, its realization is a cofibration.

## Proof.

We prove the projective case; the flat case is proved analogously.

In particular,as in the proof of Corollary1.11, we see thatηn\eta_{n}is a level

To show that it is a flat cofibration we will use
Corollary1.11. In particular it suffices to show the
following two statements:
- (1)

If a simplex in(Lnℛ​K​(𝒟∙))(L^{\mathcal{R}}_{n}K(\mathcal{D}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}))isjj-trivial (as a simplex inK​(𝒟n)mK(\mathcal{D}_{n})_{m}) then it was already inΛm​(Lnℛ​K​(𝒟∙))\Lambda_{m}(L^{\mathcal{R}}_{n}K(\mathcal{D}_{{\raisebox{0.60275pt}{\scalebox{0.5}{$\bullet$}}}}))—i.e. that
it isjj-trivial. This is tautologically true.
- (2)

If a simplex inK′​(𝒞n)mK^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{n})_{m}isjj-trivial when considered as a simplex
inK′​(𝒟n)mK^{\prime}(\mathcal{D}_{n})_{m}then it isjj-trivial as a simplex inK′​(𝒞n)mK^{\prime}(\mathcal{C}_{n})_{m}. This
is also true, sincemm-triviality depends only on the underlying
categorical structure of each simplex.

Taken together, these two statements give exactly the condition in
Corollary1.11, so we see thatηn\eta_{n}is flat, as desired.
∎

## 4.Increasing spectra vs flat spectra

In this section we give the technical ingredients and the main steps of the
proof that increasing spectra are flat. Checking that the objects and maps are
well-defined is lengthy and annoying, and is thus postponed to
Section5.

## 4.1.An explicit model of the smash product

Here we give an explicit construction of the smash product of two symmetric
spectra.

It suffices to check that for all(a,b)∈𝒯n(a,b)\in\mathscr{T}_{n}, the category𝒞=(a,b)/𝒯n≤1\mathcal{C}=(a,b)/\mathscr{T}_{n}^{\leq 1}is connected. This is the category of all pairs(x,y)(x,y)such thatn−1≤x+y≤nn-1\leq x+y\leq nandx≥ax\geq a,y≥by\geq b. This is

connected. By definition,colim𝒯n≤1Pn,X,Y=coeq⁡(α,β).\operatorname*{colim}_{\mathscr{T}^{\leq 1}_{n}}P_{n,X,Y}=\operatorname{coeq}(\alpha,\beta).

Thus Schwede’s definition of(X∧Y)n(X\wedge Y)_{n}is exactlycolim𝒯n≤1P\operatorname*{colim}_{\mathscr{T}^{\leq 1}_{n}}P. In this paper we will instead use the definition(4.2)(X∧Y)n=colim𝒯nPn,X,Y.(X\wedge Y)_{n}=\operatorname*{colim}_{\mathscr{T}_{n}}P_{n,X,Y}.

Since the inclusion of𝒯n≤1\mathscr{T}^{\leq 1}_{n}is final, these two are naturally
isomorphic, but our definition will be more useful to us in our analysis of
latching spaces.

We can therefore conclude that(4.3)LnT​X≅colimPn,X,𝕊¯.L^{T}_{n}X\cong\operatorname*{colim}P_{n,X,\overline{\mathbb{S}}}.

## 4.2.Flat spectra are increasing

In this subsection we prove that all flat spectra are increasing.

## Proposition 4.9.

All flat spectra are increasing.

## Proof.

LetXXbe a flat spectrum. We must prove, for alln≥1n\geq 1, that

## 4.3.Roots

In an increasing symmetric spectrum, latched and unlatched simplices behave
similarly to degenerate and nondegenerate simplices in a simplicial set, in that
every latched simplex is latched to auniqueunlatched simplex, which we
call itsroot. The goal of this section is to prove this fact.

We begin by showing that latching is a transitive relation on simplices.

Latching is a transitive relation on simplices ofXX.

## Lemma 4.11.

Latching is transitive. In other words, letk<m<nk<m<n, and letz∈Xkz\in X_{k},y∈Xmy\in X_{m}andx∈Xnx\in X_{n}. Letδ∈Sm−k\delta\in S^{m-k}andϵ∈Sn−m\epsilon\in S^{n-m}.
Ifyyis(δ,τ)(\delta,\tau)-latched tozzandxxis(ϵ,ρ)(\epsilon,\rho)-latched
toyythenxxis(δ∧ϵ,ρ​(τ×1))(\delta\wedge\epsilon,\rho(\tau\times 1))-latched tozz.

## Proof.

We havex\displaystyle x=ρ⋅σn−m​(y,ϵ)=ρ⋅σn−m​(τ⋅σ​m−k​(z,δ),ϵ)\displaystyle=\rho\cdot\sigma_{n-m}(y,\epsilon)=\rho\cdot\sigma_{n-m}(\tau\cdot\sigma{m-k}(z,\delta),\epsilon)=ρ​(τ×1)​σn−m​(σm−k​(z,δ),ϵ)=ρ​(τ×1)​σn−k​(z,δ∧ϵ),\displaystyle=\rho(\tau\times 1)\sigma_{n-m}(\sigma_{m-k}(z,\delta),\epsilon)=\rho(\tau\times 1)\sigma_{n-k}(z,\delta\wedge\epsilon),

as desired.
∎

For a simplexxxinXnX_{n}, let𝒫x\mathcal{P}_{x}be the poset of simplicesyythatxxis latched to, under
the orderingy<y′y<y^{\prime}ify′y^{\prime}is latched toyy. This poset is graded with|y|=k|y|=kify∈Xky\in X_{k}, and ify≤y′y\leq y^{\prime}then|y|=|y′||y|=|y^{\prime}|if and only ify=y′y=y^{\prime}.

## Lemma 4.12.

If simplicesy,y′y,y^{\prime}have an upper boundwwin𝒫x\mathcal{P}_{x}then there exists a lower boundzzwithmax⁡(|w|−|y|,|w|−|y′|)≥max⁡(|y|−|z|,|y′|−|z|)\max(|w|-|y|,|w|-|y^{\prime}|)\geq\max(|y|-|z|,|y^{\prime}|-|z|).

## Proof.

We prove this by induction onmax⁡(|w|−|y|,|w|−|y′|)\max(|w|-|y|,|w|-|y^{\prime}|).

Ifmax⁡(|w|−|y|,|w|−|y′|)=0\max(|w|-|y|,|w|-|y^{\prime}|)=0, so thatw=y=y′w=y=y^{\prime}then the claim trivially holds withz=wz=w.

thus there exists a lower boundzzforyyandz′z^{\prime}such thatmax⁡(|y|−|z|,|z′|−|z|)≤max⁡(|y1|−|y|,|y1|−|z′|)<M\max(|y|-|z|,|z^{\prime}|-|z|)\leq\max(|y_{1}|-|y|,|y_{1}|-|z^{\prime}|)<M. Thuszzis

both. Then the previously-handled case applies to the pairyyandz′z^{\prime}(which have a common upper bound ofyM−1y_{M-1}, giving a lower boundz′′z^{\prime\prime}with|y|−|z′′|=1|y|-|z^{\prime\prime}|=1and|z′|−|z′′|≤M−1|z^{\prime}|-|z^{\prime\prime}|\leq M-1). Then the previous case also applies
to the pairz′′z^{\prime\prime}andy′y^{\prime}(which have a common upper bound ofyM−1′y^{\prime}_{M-1},
giving a lower boundzzwith|z′′|−|z|≤M−1|z^{\prime\prime}|-|z|\leq M-1and|y′|−|z|≤M|y^{\prime}|-|z|\leq M).
Since|y|−|z′′|=1|y|-|z^{\prime\prime}|=1, this implies thatzzis a lower bound with|y|−|z|≤M|y|-|z|\leq M, as well, as desired.
∎

## Lemma 4.13.

IfXXis increasing then for every simplexx∈Xnx\in X_{n}there exists a unique
maximalkk, together with unique simplexy∈Xn−ky\in X_{n-k}and(τ,δ)(\tau,\delta)such thatxxis(τ,δ)(\tau,\delta)-latched toyy.

## Proof.

Since gradings must be nonnegative integers, Lemma4.12implies the poset𝒫x\mathcal{P}_{x}must have a minimal
element, and thus there is a unique simplex with maximalkk.
∎

LetXXbe an increasing spectrum.
Letx∈Xnx\in X_{n}and lety∈Xky\in X_{k}be the root ofxx. If(τ,δ)(\tau,\delta)and(τ′,δ′)(\tau^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})are such thatxxis(τ,δ)(\tau,\delta)- and(τ′,δ′)(\tau^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})-latched toyy, then(τ,δ)=(τ′,δ′)(\tau,\delta)=(\tau^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}).

Our goal is to show that the root ofxxis the initial object of𝒞x\mathcal{C}_{x}.

We denote by(τx,δx)(\tau_{x},\delta_{x})the unique shuffleτx∈Sn−k,k\tau_{x}\in S_{n-k,k}and simplexδx∈Sk\delta_{x}\in S^{k}such thatxxis(τx,δx)(\tau_{x},\delta_{x})-latched torxr_{x}. Ifxxis unlatched we definerx=xr_{x}=x,τx=1∈Sn,0\tau_{x}=1\in S_{n,0}andδx=∗\delta_{x}=*.

## 4.4.The combinatorial latching space

We now give an alternate combinatorial description of a latching spaceLnC​XL^{C}_{n}Xusing
roots. Since roots are not well-defined for non-increasing spectra, this
definitiononlyholds whenXXis increasing. It takes significant work
to check thatLnC​XL^{C}_{n}Xis well-defined, so this will be postponed to
Section5.

## Definition 4.17.

LetXXbe an increasing spectrum, and letn≥0n\geq 0. WriteS(k,n−k)⊆ΣnS_{(k,n-k)}\subseteq\Sigma_{n}for the set of(k,n−k)(k,n-k)-shuffles. ## 4.5.Proofs of the main results

We are now ready to prove
Theorem1.14,
Corollary1.15, and
that increasing spectra are flat.

## Proof of Theorem1.14.

By Proposition4.9XXis increasing.
Using Proposition4.21we can consider the mapνC\nu^{C}rather thanνT\nu^{T}. SinceXXis flat,νT\nu^{T}is injective. Then the result follows from the characterization of projective cofibrations in Remark1.4.
∎

## Proof of Corollary1.15.

IfYYis projective for alln,m≥0n,m\geq 0, Theorem1.14implies the action ofΣn\Sigma_{n}on(Yn)m∖(Λn​Y)m(Y_{n})_{m}\smallsetminus(\Lambda_{n}Y)_{m}

is free.
Sinceffis
flatνn\nu_{n}is a cofibration. The action ofΣn\Sigma_{n}on(Yn)m∖(Λn​Y)m(Y_{n})_{m}\smallsetminus(\Lambda_{n}Y)_{m}is free, so the action ofΣn\Sigma_{n}on the subset(Yn)m∖(Λn​Y∪Λn​Xfn​(Xn))m(Y_{n})_{m}\smallsetminus(\Lambda_{n}Y\cup_{\Lambda_{n}X}f_{n}(X_{n}))_{m}

is free.
∎

## Proposition 4.22.

All increasing spectra are flat.

## Proof.

Using Proposition4.21it suffices to show thatνnC\nu_{n}^{C}is
injective. We prove this by contradiction.

Assume that there existmm-simplices(γ,x,δ)(\gamma,x,\delta)and(γ′,x′,δ′)(\gamma^{\prime},x^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})withx∈Xm−kx\in X_{m-k}andx′∈Xm−k′x^{\prime}\in X_{m-k^{\prime}}such thatνnC​(γ,x,δ)=νnC​(γ′,x′,δ′)\nu^{C}_{n}(\gamma,x,\delta)=\nu^{C}_{n}(\gamma^{\prime},x^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}). Writey=νnC​(γ,x,δ)y=\nu_{n}^{C}(\gamma,x,\delta). Then by definition,y=γ⋅σk​(x,δ)=γ′⋅σk′​(x′,δ′).y=\gamma\cdot\sigma_{k}(x,\delta)=\gamma^{\prime}\cdot\sigma_{k^{\prime}}(x^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}).

Thusxxis the root ofyy, withyy(γ,δ)(\gamma,\delta)-latched toxx. But it
is also the case thatx′x^{\prime}is the root ofyy, withyy(γ′,δ′)(\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime})-latched tox′x^{\prime}. By Lemma4.13,x=x′x=x^{\prime}and by Lemma4.16(γ,δ)=(γ′,δ′)(\gamma,\delta)=(\gamma^{\prime},\delta^{\prime}).
ThusνnC\nu_{n}^{C}is monic, as desired.
∎

## Lemma 5.27.

IfXXis increasing,μn\mu_{n}maps simplices whoseXX-component is unlatched to
distinct simplices.

Sinceγ1−1​γ2​(m)≠m\gamma_{1}^{-1}\gamma_{2}(m)\neq m, by1.12(2) there exists a simplex which bothx1x_{1}andx2x_{2}are latched to. This is a contradiction, since we assumed that bothx1x_{1}andx2x_{2}are unlatched. Thus we must haveγ1=γ2\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{2}, so

Now suppose that we havek1<k2k_{1}<k_{2}, and(γi,xi,δi)(\gamma_{i},x_{i},\delta_{i})withxix_{i}unlatched, such thatφk1​(γ1,x1,δ1)=φk2​(γ2,x2,δ2).\varphi_{k_{1}}(\gamma_{1},x_{1},\delta_{1})=\varphi_{k_{2}}(\gamma_{2},x_{2},\delta_{2}).
